ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-21.ana.ud1877e190	When the Storting will now discuss the Communication on a comprehensive integration policy, it is possible to choose different approaches. We can start with the positive – what makes integration actually work better in Norway than in many other countries. We can have the main focus on success criteria and see how we can get more out of what actually works, or we can pick the opposite starting point, look with light and light after what has not worked fully as well, and dig down there. As rapporteur of the case, I choose the first one, as the message also does. It's the positive results that give the most truthful picture of today's diverse Norwegian societies, diversity and communities, or diversity and opportunities that my party's national meeting called it. That doesn't mean that everything is perfect, then we wouldn't need this big message. Everyone knows that diversity also poses challenges. But choosing a negative intake with a one-sided focus on what doesn't work gives us a distorted view of reality and doesn't bring us any further. It is a quality sign of Norwegian policy when almost the entire Committee emphasises that diversity and community are important for individuals, and at least equally important for society as a whole. In addition, there we are fully in line with popular opinion, as the Statistical Central Bureau measures every year, and from a positive point of view continues to move in a positive direction. A large majority of the Norwegian population regard immigrants as an important resource for Norwegian society, both in working life and with what they provide from their own culture. Then you can ask yourself what makes the level of conflict in Norwegian society as low as it is. The answer is probably relatively obvious. We're a society with little differences between people. We have a broad consensus on important values such as democracy, freedom of expression, equality and equivalency. A general objective of integration policy is for immigrants coming to Norway to experience respect and as part of the community with the duties and rights of other residents in Norway. This has meant that we, better than many others, have in a very short time managed to deal with relatively large population changes. In 1970, only 1.5 pst. by the Norwegian population, the background of migration. Today, this is increasing to over 13p.p., with a background from 219 countries and autonomous regions. By comparison, the number of UN member states is 193. The majority stress that successful integration is based on reciprocity in rights and obligations, that it is crucial for good social development that citizens are able to take responsibility for their own lives. The primary responsibility for this will always lie with the individual, but the authorities must at the same time contribute to giving new citizens a chance to become part of Norwegian society. Integration is a two-way process, based on rights and obligations. It is about building barriers where they may be found, in education, working life, health, provision of services, etc. The barriers may be high or low, depending on the individual’s starting point. There is a difference between coming to Norway as a migrant worker, a family immigrant or a refugee. It is different for adults than for children; there are differences between men and women, boys and girls. We have the whole range from higher-educated to illiterate. There are differences within and between immigrants from different countries and continents. Therefore, measures must also be flexible, as individualised as possible and cover all life phases. This is a good message and an important message. There is a point of view shared by the 16 organisations that participated in the Stortingen hearing. The government here demonstrates how the large and small integration programmes and measures are interrelated across sectors. The responsibility for implementing the different measures for languages, work, education, housing, leisure activities and other social participation lies in the different sectors, but people do not live their lives according to the sector principle. People live across sectors, every day, all their lives. They switch between the public, private and voluntary sectors. They move between national, regional and local levels, and last but not least – all live in a municipality. One of the most important success criteria for successful integration is therefore that we are able to see different actions in context and do things in the right order. Once the Ministry of Children, Gender Equality and Inclusion has been given responsibility for coordinating and ensuring an integrated integration policy, that responsibility must also be real. It's not just about writing a message, it's about appropriations and concrete implementation. The majority believe that the main track in such a comprehensive integration policy is language, housing, education and work. Language is fundamental to the rest. That's why we've doubled the number of hours for them with the right and duty to Norwegian and social knowledge. Therefore, we will review the introduction scheme and propose improvements in it. Therefore, the majority would like to have possible extensions of the personality assessed for Norwegian training. This is why we have trials with free core time in kindergarten, so that all children should be equally well equipped to take hold on the first day of school. Therefore, we want language mapping and language stimulation to be gradually expanded from today's 90 pst. to include all children. That's why we have introduced free homework to mention something. The best way to learn languages is to practice it with the Nordics. In this context, we should not underestimate the value of voluntary organisational life. Many organisations have developed good offers, partly funded by government grants, but here there is room for expansion, which was clearly highlighted in our consultation. Work is important in all ways. For each individual, it is about the ability to contribute with their abilities and qualifications, the freedom to obtain housing, the freedom to live independently, to participate in leisure activities. Work is the main means of combating poverty. We know that immigrants and children with a migrant background are overrepresented among people with persistently low incomes. Our welfare society depends on the values created by work. In Norway, we have a high level of employment, both among men and women, and among immigrants and the rest of the population. But that's a difference. There is a greater difference between men and women among immigrants than elsewhere in the population. Figures show that if we are able to close the employment gap, we have covered one third of our labour demand by 2060. One of several measures is to improve the recognition system for training and real competence from abroad. Another measure is the transformation of the New Chance Programme into Job Opportunities, with particular emphasis on the qualification of women who are far from the labour market. A third measure is attempts at the allocation of immigrants to state jobs. A fourth measure is closer cooperation between Nav and employers to increase the number of immigrants for interview and employment. We need good role models that can help remove barriers created by discriminatory attitudes, and perhaps especially those that are not entirely conscious. Since 2005, 330,000 new jobs have been created in Norway, most of them in the private sector. Immigrants make important contributions to this. 21 pst. by new establishments in 2011 were established by immigrants. This needs to be encouraged more. That's why we want the founding guidance for immigrants into solid forms. In some environments we know that there is social control of young people, especially girls, but also boys, and also adult women. Education and work are the best way out of oppression and into independence and control over your own life. Norwegian women know all about that. We must also be able to share that knowledge with our new sisters. It is positive that immigrant girls are now storming into higher and higher education. Hopefully, this will also eventually lead to them storming into the labour market and pulling more of them with them. But at the same time, we need to build up barriers for those who need extra measures to get there. It's never too late. There are many good examples of this, including in adult immigrant women. Nor should we forget that some immigrant boys are not doing as well in school as the girls. Some health comments: The government points out that an equivalent health offer means that everyone has to be treated differently according to their needs. That's good, but there's also a challenge here. We know that there are social differences in health. We know that some disorders occur more frequently among immigrants than others, but one of the main problems is that our knowledge is fragmented and has many holes. Therefore, it is positive that there will soon be a public health impact report, where specific challenges for immigrants are addressed, and in the wake of it, a national strategy for immigrant health. In the end, I would like to stress once again the positive fact that the Storting is as united as we are – not completely unanimous, but as well as united – when it comes to seeing the possibilities of diversity. There are some minority proposals that I assume are presented and promoted by the minority itself. And then I look forward to a constructive debate on a very important topic.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-21.ana.ud1877e222	I would first like to thank the case rapporteur for a good review of the matter. As the rapporteur also says, there is a broad consensus in the Committee on Part of the Communication. Many people have waited a long time and with an interest in the government now providing a big message on integration in Norway. Many people have been waiting to see what works, what doesn't work, and what can be done to make things that don't work can get better. In this way, the message has partly responded to expectations and partly not. There is a great deal of glorification in the communication about Norwegian integration policy, and in many ways it can seem that most of it works very well, and that Norwegian integration policy is successful. Norwegian integration is very good – it has to be a backtrace. But there is also a lot that is not good, and it also points to the message. It's important to focus on what's not good, because it's something you have to do with. The government parties have pointed to sunshine stories about everything that is successful, and what is successful is also the Advance Party's agreement that is good. But it's part of the opposition's task to put your finger on what's less good, and to challenge the government if they really want to do something about this. Successful integration is about how immigrants should become equivalent contributors in the Norwegian community and find security in a society based on essential values of freedom and interpersonal trust. Successful integration is also about major economic values in Norwegian society, a society in which people contribute and participate. Successful integration is about using the right tools to get good results. Norway has spent 20 years on measures with little or no effect. We have to venture to use what works. Successful integration is also about having a sustainable immigration policy, a policy that ensures that Norwegian values are respected and further developed, so that Norway can continue to be a welfare society. In political Norway, the sense is somewhat evenly distributed, and I appreciate my political opponents when they do or say something that I think makes sense. I would like to refer to a political opponent, namely Minister of Labour Anniken Huitfeldt, who recently said something very wise. She said in the evening post on compulsory activity for social workers that making demands is a way of showing care. I very much agree with that. That's what we in the Advancement Party have been saying all these years, and we've been getting a lot off of it, both from this and other pulpits. But it does not detract Huitfeldt momentum in any way. She has probably met criticism from her own party traps and members in her cooperation parties, but what she probably asks about, and which I also ask about here and now, is: why should we be afraid to make demands? It's both right and reasonable to make demands. This also applies to those who come here to seek asylum, work or for other reasons. Investigations, consultation rounds and assessments and looking at things are well and good in some cases, but if one is to have results, one actually has to do something concrete. Action plans are also good, but they have to be concrete, they have to be used, and not least they have to be evaluated, so that someone knows what works in the plan and what does not work. Using the wrong medicine for a long time doesn't work, and it's not good. It makes the situation worse. But as long as you don't evaluate the measures, someone who said you don't know what the right medicine is to use. Seeking asylum in a country is a human right. Getting a family reunion and looking for a job is also allowed. Norway must fulfil its international obligations, and we must help refugees who have been persecuted in their original country of origin. When people seek asylum – or work, for that matter – in Norway, I assume that those who come to Norway come here because they want to live in Norway. There are many countries in the world to which you can come. Some countries are a little more like the country from which they come, both climate and culturally. It must be allowed to believe that when a person chooses to come to a country far from his own, both geographically and culturally, it is because one wants to be part of that country, and it must be allowed to expect that those who come are willing to adapt to Norway. It can't be too much of a requirement. It is not possible to undermine the fact that immigration creates some challenges and that some people have greater difficulties in being integrated than others. The fact that immigration into Norway has been large, perhaps too large, may be one of the reasons why integration creates these challenges. When more come to the country than the country manages to receive in a good way, it creates problems. When one continues to immigrate while thousands are in asylum reception waiting to settle in a municipality, the problems will only increase. There are today more than 15 000 people in Norwegian asylum recipients. Only in 2012 nearly 9,800 people sought asylum in Norway. In addition, of course, there is family immigration and labour immigration. These are relatively high numbers. When one at the same time knows that many municipalities do not have the capacity to accept and settle immigrants due to lack of housing, lack of jobs and lack of school places, and lack of capacity to meet health and social demands, this situation is exacerbating. One must remember that it is not the government that integrates immigrants, nor is the Stortinget; it is the municipalities and those who live there. Norway is the country that receives most immigrants in relation to the population. Maybe it's time we put on the brakes a little bit for a while, so we can take better care of the ones that are already here, instead of filling in with even more. We may have reached a saturation point where the municipalities do not have the power to receive more, and that is why the settlement is slower. Many who come to Norway cannot read or write – they are illiterate. They don't have any education either. These people are facing greater challenges of integration. They're going to learn a foreign language, they're going to learn to read and write, they're going to get an education, and they're going to try to get to work. Many of these do little or none of this. They therefore end up as recipients of welfare benefits and thus become a burden on society, rather than a source of enrichment. In the communication, the government says that immigrants are a resource and that the employment rate is evidence. The fact is that more and more of those who have come to work as immigrants and have remained in the country end up in job queues and become recipients of social security benefits. The refresher has studied this and come to some interesting findings. A study shows, among other things: that immigrants from Lithuania and Poland who came to Norway in the period 2003–2007 and who were given a large number of jobs in the construction industry were found to be far more unemployed than Norwegians, and that they remained unemployed to a much greater extent than comparable Norwegians. Previous studies show that Pakistani and Turks who came to Norway in the 1970s disappeared rather quickly from the labour market. After only ten years in Norway, these were overrepresented in social security statistics. According to the study, similar features are found in new groups from non-European countries. The study showed that the unemployment rate among immigrants was high, up to 18 pst. What is worrying is that the unemployment rate among these people is increasing, and that it's keeping high. Then it turns out that at the same time as those who came first, and who went out on leave, there came a lot of new ones, which went straight to work. It's worrying, as it's easy to imagine that these too can end up on the statistics, and that there might be some cynicism in the world of work. The refresher centre points to several explanations of this phenomenon. Language problems, poor contact networks, that they are less adaptable, and that they have lower formal education, are some of the points. Then, of course, the Norwegian welfare systems come as a possible explanation. This will in the long run be unsustainable for Norwegian society. There will then be fewer and fewer people paying the well-being goods to an increasing number of them. This shows that immigration is not entirely successful and not entirely sustainable. The SSB describes this through its 15/2015 report. I quote: ‘A sustained increase in immigration from EU countries, America and Australia will to some extent ease the pressure on public finances out of this century through slower ageing of the population and increased tax revenues. Immigration from low-income countries, on the other hand, weakens public finances. In the very long term, migrants who have the same economic behaviour as current non-migrants will not solve the funding challenges of the welfare country, as they will also use the welfare systems. Professional activity is essential for development.’ The fact that participation in the labour market is an important factor for integration is a consensus. Another important factor is language and social understanding. Manages a non-language, will be out of work and outside society. The Progress Party will show that too many people who have the right and duty to learn Norwegian and social knowledge are not at work or under education. A compilation of the status of course participants from the years 2005 to 2009 shows that about 40 pst. the participants were not in the job following the end of the programme. This is objectionable. There is also a clear indication that the integration of immigrants is not working well enough on a very close basis, and that saying that integration is successful rests on some failing basis. The Progress Party considers it important to monitor the competence of individual immigrants and to ensure that any additional education, education or training is provided, in order to get into profitable work to become an economic self-wheeler. The Progress Party clearly sees a 40-pst exclusion from the labour market. participants in the introduction system are a serious societal problem and those outside have to be caught. There are many reasons for this non-response. We believe that the absence of a multidisciplinary approach to catch up with these people in the long term will lead to their falling outside the Norwegian community. In my view and in the view of the Progress Party, it is clear that in such cases one has to make strong demands and that public benefits are to be paid only against compulsory employment. The notification also states that languages are important and an overall committee believes that the identification of languages at the earliest possible stage is an important tool for providing good language training. Learning the language is important. The introduction system now has 600 hours of language training. The problem is only that it is mandatory to attend and not to learn. There are therefore many who complete the introduction system without being able to speak Norwegian. This is unacceptable in the opinion of the Progress Party. The government suggests that a person should take a social test, but that he can be taken in his own native language. I mean, that's a record bomb. Social skills and language training must be seen in the context. Language training and understanding must be required. Those who are to stay in Norway must be able to take a test in language and social knowledge. Grades must be set, which means something about the knowledge of individual immigrants. This will also serve as a diploma to employers on a later occasion. The integration message is comprehensive in terms of words, but slightly poor in terms of action and action. The government has spent eight years trying to figure out what to evaluate or figure out. We can count in the message: The government will assess 19 actions, address nine issues and send out to the hearing one poor little thing. Action plans that have not been evaluated in 20 years, investigations and good wishes do little to help achieve a more successful integration. Only concrete measures are helpful. There is still a lot to be done before one can say that integration in Norway is successful, and one thing I am sure: this message does not end the integration debate in Norway. The working party says everyone's coming, and I say, "When then?" I would like to end by addressing the proposals that the Progress Party has made in the vote, either alone or with others.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-21.ana.ud1877e278	The representative of Morten Ørsal Johansen has taken up the suggestions he referred to.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-21.ana.ud1877e281	It's an important big message we're handling today. In 1982, in a large report, the SSB predicted that the population of Norway would start to decline in 2016, after reaching the peak of 4.3 million inhabitants. In Oslo, the population would go down because of an ageing population. The reality was quite different, last year we passed five million inhabitants, and Oslo is the capital of Europe that is growing strongest. What's happened? We have been given increasing birth rates due to changes in family policy, and we have had a strong growth in immigration, especially in labour migration from the EEA region. Therefore, in my article I want to focus most on what the government has focused at least on in its message, namely, the immigrants at work. Immigration through family reunification, refugees and asylum seekers is duly presented by the case rapporteur, and a large majority of the population in the United Kingdom join most of the government's conclusions in these areas. Language, education and work are the most important factors for successful integration, and it is particularly important to focus on women in this context. The need and opportunities for cross-border movement of persons increase as a result of increased world trade, reduced transport costs, reduction of border barriers and increased trade between countries. The right considers increased labour migration to be a contribution to taking these developments seriously and exploiting the potential of globalisation. We are critical that the notification places very little emphasis on the conditions of integration affected by labour immigration into Norway. We do not have an overview of the sectors and occupations in which labour will be needed in the coming years, what information measures have been taken to ensure good information for the increasing flow of labour migrants from EEA countries, what skills are being sought today, and what tools should be used to employ those who are not currently part of the labour market. We see the need to investigate which criteria ensure success in terms of multicultural recruitment. The labour market is likely to be characterised by increasing migration of workers and service providers over the years to come, but also by increased competition for labour between countries. Having a conscious and comprehensive labour immigration policy is one of the main policy challenges in the years to come. Knowledge and competence, together with diversity in the workforce, are crucial for innovation and industrial development in an increasingly competitive and international labour and business. In order to maintain the current level of well-being and ensure value creation through a living knowledge-based economy, Norway needs to pursue a policy that enables us to compete internationally for the competent labour force. The main objective of labour immigration policy is to facilitate the recruitment of foreign labour by enterprises and businesses in a simple and efficient way when they need it. At the same time, frameworks and measures should be designed to ensure that the benefits resulting from labour migration can be achieved in a balanced way for employers, individuals and society. Every single migrant worker shall be guaranteed good working and social conditions. The fact that there is work to be done, and that it is well paid, is absolutely essential for workers in other countries to consider it relevant to seek employment in Norway. It is important to ensure better information for immigrants, preferably in more languages than today. Prior to the financial crisis, workers from other countries with the necessary workforce contributed, inter alia: construction and repair industries and in several service industries. Without this extra labour available, growth in the Norwegian economy would have slowed down. At the same time, we see that several of these immigrants have rotted in Norway, and families have moved after. Then it is important to have the opportunity to learn the language properly, either through work or through a course. In addition, there is a need for more targeted measures to attract specific competences to Norway. The right sees that it is not obvious that the competent labour force is applying to Norway. We pay relatively low wages for academics, specialists and researchers and have a compressed pay structure. It is important to market Norway for foreign labour. Although Norway is an attractive country for those with low qualifications, the highly qualified are looking for a better place to pay for their skills. This issue needs to be taken seriously if Norway is to be able to maintain and further develop the welfare society of Norway to become a knowledge nation in the future. Consideration should be given to the means of increasing the migration of skilled labour not only from EU countries but also from the rest of the world. Today, Norway is losing this competition in relation to countries such as the United States, Canada and the United Kingdom, despite Norway being able to live in the world’s best country annually. Today, the strict requirements must be met for foreign nationals who do not come from the EEA territory to receive a work permit. Since 1975, Norway has had limited and regulated immigration. This means that non-residents cannot move to Norway and take up work here unless the non-resident fulfils the conditions of one of the derogations. All non-residents who intend to take up work or pursue their own business must be authorised to work. The right considers that businesses should be eligible for prior approval in order to allow professionals/specialists to start their work there while the Foreign Affairs Directorate, UDI, is examining the application. A rapid track system should be established for pre-authorised enterprises. Today's regulation regulates who is qualified as a specialist, so as to fall within the scheme. It should be left to employers/enterprise to assess for themselves whether an employee has the right skills. It will eliminate bureaucracy and release resources to deal with more complex issues. Authorisation to work as a professional may be granted for up to three years, but no longer than the duration of the employment relationship. Renewal is a very bureaucratic process, which takes a lot of time and gives long periods of unresolved situation and stress to those involved. It is necessary to simplify these control activities, in particular those previously authorised. Therefore, the right-hand side proposes that a model with prior approval of companies be examined. Only 5 pst. by immigrants who apply to EU countries or Europe in general, have higher education or are skilled, against more than 50 pst. by those who are granted a residence permit in the United States and Canada. A comprehensive change in labour immigration policy should therefore be considered by looking at the experience gained in the field between the EU and the United States. We have previously proposed the introduction of a so-called 'Sheet Card' similar to the EU countries, using the model of the US 'Green Card'. It is crucial for the sustainability of the welfare model that Norway is able to continue a higher share of value-creating and tax-paying inhabitants. At the same time, we need to ensure that those who are actually at work do not become dependent on sustainable grants in order to maintain income they can live on in Norway. Even the best welfare society depends on the financing of profits in the productive sector. It is important that as many people as possible participate in the labour market, while joining forces to ensure basic security for those who are unable to work. The basic starting point for the Right is that each one takes personal responsibility for himself, his own life and for the people around him. At the same time, a voice and human society must ensure all opportunities for a decent life. The basis for well-being is that it must pay off to work, while ensuring good and decent support for those who are too sick to participate in the labour market, but even if they are to have good income insurance schemes, they must always pay off to work. We want a special focus on getting more immigrant women into work and in active participation in society. According to the SSB, at least 8,600 immigrants live in Norway aged 16 and over who have not completed any education at all. About 5,600 of these are women. In addition to the fact that there are much more women without education among immigrants, there are also relatively more immigrant women with short higher education. New opportunities, introduced by the Bondevik II government, are a pilot scheme aimed at testing and documenting experiences with different methods of getting more immigrants into work or education. There is a need to strengthen the activity of economic social assistance, especially for the young social clients group. No young person who does not have significantly reduced health should be able to carry out passive public transfers. The person coming to the municipal social services shall meet with an offer and an expectation of participation in work or work-clearing activities. There is considerable scope for both demanding activity and cutting the benefit if the requirement is not complied with in Section 20 of the Social Services Act. We believe that greater efforts are needed towards young people, and that priority is given to efforts directed at young social clients who may later end up in disability. We want a door-to-door ‘snow’ practice at all Nav offices throughout Norway. It is necessary to require those receiving public support, while facilitating participation. Mastering the language is essential for good integration. The motivation among immigrants to learn Norwegian is often greater than the public language training offer. We need to strengthen Norwegian training and to facilitate the recognition of the mother tongue of immigrants as a second foreign language. Language training should also become a right for migrant workers from EEA countries, and unemployment should not exclude one from language training. Such training will provide job-seekers with additional qualifications and may help them to enter the labour market more quickly. A number of non-governmental organisations, such as Red Cross and Caritas, are doing important work in this area. We will underline the importance of a good interaction between the public, private and voluntary sectors. I then take up the Right's proposal in this matter.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-21.ana.ud1877e336	The representative of Trond Helleland has taken up the suggestions he referred to.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-21.ana.ud1877e339	First of all, I would like to thank the Committee and not least the rapporteur and the committee secretary for their very wise and thorough work on this position. I was one of those who in their time walked around with the colorful T-shirt with ‘Yes to a colorful community’ and the button with ‘Not my bullying comrade’. I wonder when that was faith. I went into this internet, as we say in the interior, and there I found sociologist Sigurd Skirbeck, who could help me with that. He points out that in 1987 there was an advertisement printed in the evening post with the text: ‘Defense yourself, your people and future generations against future-blind politicians.’ The advertisement characterised the information on Norwegian immigration policy as opinion fascism. The evening post took in the ad, the Arbeiderblad did not do it, and wrote in the management position: ‘It is a shame that we in Norway, with such a small migration and refugee problem, are not able to solve these questions in a morally high-level manner.’ The next day there was a large meeting in Jern and Metall Oslo, where Reulf Steen, according to the newspaper report, among others. pronounced: «Rasism is a disease that we can all be affected by. We are all disposed of to become racists, under certain conditions and circumstances." Ellen Stensrud, who was then the leader of Iron and Metal Oslo, followed up by saying: «As long as we have financial problems, more people will be infected. It is therefore important that we, like the occupational health service, do preventive work. Otherwise, I fear that the disease will break far into our ranks." Then there was an impressive wide mobilization, and the campaign, as I have already mentioned, ‘Yes to a colorful community’, was launched by the Red Cross and got cross-political excitement. Not least in school environments, jacket tags were distributed with the text: ‘Not my bullying comrade.’ I had two daughters who, certainly on a voluntary basis, walked around with these marks on them. It was at the end of the 1980s that non-European immigration became noticeable in our country, at least for those of us who lived in central urban areas. It's also in this period that we find the first articulate and organized resistance to the same immigration. Not least it was in the media we met this. Instead of a factual and knowledge-based approach, the media bombarded their readers, hearers and viewers with a number of shock-carded individual pisodes. According to Skirbeck, the message was that it was a disease that was in the process of infecting Norway and, as Skirbeck wrote, that the only decent response that was prescribed was that one had to try to isolate the same disease and ‘stop infection from spreading’. In retrospect, Skirbeck says that if a reader of Norwegian newspapers from this time on, it seems that the fight against AIDS and the fight against racism will be met in the same way. This is really embarrassing to read now in retrospect, a quarter of a century later. In these 25 years we have had immigration. Everything indicates that it will continue, and that we actually want it to continue. This message, and not least the configuration, shows it in a very good way. There's a large majority in this house, and I'm convinced that it's the same thing on the outside, that wants immigration, and that this has largely gone so well. We have become a richer country in many meanings of the word, a richer society, a more exciting society – a much more modern society, in which modern citizens thrive in living and working. Immigration is not a threat to further social development. Immigration is, on the contrary, a prerequisite for good and modern social development, and I quote from the vote, ‘The majority of the Committee, all except the members of the Progress Party’. Today, I am really extra happy not to be a big representative of the Progress Party. It must be so tiring in length to be so skeptical of everything new and of all those who do not look or sound ‘typically Norwegian’. My hope is that what still stands out in the progress party comments as, as I read it in any case, ‘cos of discontent’ expressions, will later appear as embarrassingly out of date, both valuable and not least knowledgeable. In panel debates with progress party politicians in recent times, and many of these have been in these asylum-children's times, Norwegian immigration and integration policies are almost characterised as a disaster. Expressions are used as total disc bombs. So luckily, I noticed that Morten Ørsal Johansen changed a little today. That's a good starting point. SV is concerned when we talk about immigration policy, that it is important to assume that the immigration population has some challenges related to education, skills, languages, etc. But it's so important to assume that the Norwegian society to which they migrate has some challenges. We are a class society, we have a sex labour market, we have a working life that is characterised or infected by social dumping, and we have a difficult situation on the housing market – not least for people with low incomes. We have an adult learning system that should be expanded and become stronger and better. We have a society where people with mental problems are struggling, etc. We struggle with bullying in schools, workplaces and online. Just to immigrate into such a society and live in such a society is demanding for all of us and, naturally, a little more demanding for our immigration people. This message is good. It describes and argues for both rights and obligations. Yes, it argues for obligations. The representative of Ørsal Johansen must read the message in more detail. It describes, argues for and is not least about the whole and about what Ellen Stensrud mentioned 25 years ago as preventive work. It shows in a good way that there is an equal, co-ordinated approach to this topic – all three messages indicate that immigration is a good thing, that immigration is a prerequisite, not least for development in District Norway, and the three messages are price-effectively similar in actions both in terms of housing, language, work and education. ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-21.ana.ud1877e392	First of all, I would like to thank the President-in-Office for the work that has been done well and thoroughly. Norway is part of an increasingly globalised world. Immigration has helped Norway to have a more diverse population with different languages, traditions, cultures and different competences. This development has given us many new opportunities, but it also gives us new challenges. Norway's largest resource is the people who live here. We need skills and labour, and all forecasts indicate that we are dependent on immigration to meet the labour demand in the future. At the same time, of course, it presents us with challenges, different challenges for different groups of immigrants. There are different reasons why people choose to settle in Norway. Immigrants can be divided into four main groups: those who come here to take education, refugees and asylum seekers, family immigrants and migrant workers. Those who come here to take education are the smallest group. It is a group that lives in Norway for a limited period of time, and the group that has the least challenges to address here in the country. Refugees and asylum seekers are also a relatively small group, but this is a group that is very heterogeneous and with quite different challenges. Most come to Norway and apply for a residence permit because they believe they are being persecuted in their home country. Some have higher education, speak fluent English and have good opportunities to get a job relatively quickly. Others are illiterate and, of course, have a much longer way to go before they master Norwegian and can acquire competence as the Norwegian labour market demand. Some of them come from society quite like the Norwegian, others from villages without electricity and ingested water. Some people simply have to learn to live in a Norwegian dwelling. It goes without saying that the time it takes to be integrated into the Norwegian community will be different for these different groups. The second largest group is family immigrants. Some of them have the same background as asylum seekers, others have married Norwegian citizens and come from countries that are relatively similar to Norway, and are primarily in need of language training. The majority in this group are women. The deciduous largest group of immigrants are migrant workers. The majority in the group are men, but many of them also have the family with them. They do not have the right and obligation to introduce a programme, but they have income and therefore the possibility to take language training on their own. I also believe that employers should assume a great responsibility for language training. Many immigrants have families who do not have work in Norway, and this is a large, unused labour reserve in a labour market that is sorely in need of more labour. There is enough to pick up for employers who provide good language training and ensure that they have the skills needed in the local environment. Despite large differences in background, all immigrants have one important thing in common: the need to be well integrated into the Norwegian community — with the rights and obligations of all those who choose to live here. Good integration is important for individuals, but at least equally important for society as a whole. Our society has been based over the centuries on fundamental values such as democracy, the principles of the rule of law, human rights, freedom of expression, equality and equivalency. Integration policy must be anchored in these values. The integration process thus becomes a responsibility that includes everyone – both those who live here from before, and those who come moving here. It is an important prerequisite for successful integration, where everyone is given their resources, and everyone is taking part in the community. All residents of Norway have the same rights and obligations, and it is important to pursue a policy that clarifies the link between right and duty. Everyone has to get a chance to succeed, and everyone has to meet with common demands. The main factor for good integration is undoubtedly good Norwegian knowledge. It's about kids as well as adults. For refugees and asylum seekers, the right and obligation to participate in the introduction programme is the entry key. For many, it is sufficient to acquire basic Norwegian knowledge, but not for all. The notification states that about 1000 per year coal in Norwegian training is to be regarded as illiterate. In the hearing, KS pointed out the specific challenges involved, and that they are resident in different municipalities throughout the country. Therefore, KS requests an additional grant for illiterates in Norwegian training. I have a great deal of understanding that this is a difficult issue, and that is a matter to be referred back to in budgetary procedure. Kindergarten and school are the most important areas for the integration of children and young people. That's why I want to warn against parental segregation in school. Minority parents must meet with clear expectations and demands to leave their kids with other kids and choose their own friends. The use of religious symbols in school must be regulated in the interests of children’s right to freedom of religion and equality. The use of succulent headgear in school should be banned locally, and own beans in schools, I think, should be avoided. The hearing revealed that some of the hearing bodies wanted to remove the cash support. However, from the year 2012-2013 onwards, the scheme has been reversed and no cash support is granted for young people over two years of age. The risk that cash support may have a negative impact on children’s language development or that it will help immigrant women to go home over several years has thus been eliminated. Cash support contributes to greater freedom of choice for families with children from 12 to 24 months of age. Not all kids of that age are mature to start in kindergarten, and not all parents are mature to send so little kids into kindergarten. The centre party will work for this scheme to continue. The settlement of refugees is an important and lasting task for the municipalities. The sooner a refugee is allowed to settle in a municipality, the sooner he starts an introduction programme and the path to work or education. Unfortunately, the waiting time has increased, and this is a cause for concern. Lack of suitable housing at the right time and in the right place in municipalities is often an important reason when municipalities cannot accept refugees. The House Bank has several instruments that strengthen the social housing work in municipalities, and it is important that municipalities are familiar with it. Refugees are among the target groups for these schemes. Most refugees are not in a situation where they can buy housing when they come from reception. Therefore, a municipal rental dwelling can often be the most appropriate option. The government has repeatedly reinforced the grant of rental housing, and the House Bank can provide between 20 and 40 pst. grants to housing projects for refugees and other priority groups. The housing message signals that the maximum limit of 40 pst. can be used to a greater extent. I'm glad. The Housing Communication also describes several other strategies for the faster settlement of refugees in municipalities. Some Keywords are long-term planning, good anchoring and professional use of the private rental market. I would like to stress that the voluntary line is important for the success of integration. Housing requires the availability of housing in municipalities and the provision of a good reception apparatus and sufficient competence for good language training. That is why I am pleased that we have now established a cooperation agreement with KS that facilitates a faster settlement.

They are an important arena of labour training and social participation, while their efforts against unworthy conditions, such as racism, discrimination, forced marriage, gender mutilation or anything else, are of great importance to our democracy. Municipalities should also use the potential of voluntary organisations in the qualification of the introduction participants and the potential of organisations as meeting places and the venue for the participation of refugees and their families in the communities. It is important to strengthen the efforts of non-governmental organisations through increased operational support and to ensure that the voluntary sector remains an important area of work training and social participation. Not all integration work can be managed and organised over municipal or state budgets. Some of the barriers to integration relate to attitudes, prejudices and perceptions. We have to open our eyes to ensure that immigration, as well as creating values in dollars and ears, also enriches Norwegian culture and challenges us to increased tolerance, understanding and respect for things that may be unfamiliar. Discrimination and racism push people away and out. We create an outsidership that, at best, can pass on and, in worst cases, can activate resistance – resistance to Norwegian society, to people and to culture. Thus, prejudice and racism can become a self - fulfilling prophecy. We know that people with a non-residential name are significantly less likely to be invited to a job interview. We know stories about people who get jads, racist remarks and hoarsely slinged on the street. We've all read inappropriate and outlawed racist comments in news or social media. It is a great responsibility for us to respond to such attitudes and prejudices, and we, as officials, must be forebears, taking a settlement with those who spread hatred and racism. In addition, it is also the responsibility of the media to give a proper view of the challenges, but also of the enrichment that new cultures, attitudes and norms add to society. Because it's through mind-building work, multiple meeting places, and lower fences and fences we meet and see each other and get to know each other. It's through friendship and knowledge that we cultivate the similarities over the differences. It will be our most important tool to get to a society where everyone is respected, everyone meets tolerance and everyone knows each other at home. And that's what is the goal of good integration: that Norway is a place where we don't grow the extraterrestrials, but where we grow the community and people feel included. ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-21.ana.ud1877e627	The word integration can be a very clear term, and it's also used quite unambiguously. Of course, it has an interest far beyond the linguistic ones. Integration has become – I dare say – an integral part of the integration debate. There is a certain consensus on three broad concepts to describe the proximity or distance between a majority population and the minority population. It is first of all assimilation, which comes from Latin: equalizing, which means that immigrants in social, cultural, economic and political conditions become just like the majority – voluntary or forced, very often with emphasis on the latter. Secondly, we have integration, which can once again be divided into a pluralistic integration, where minority groups and the majority population adapt to each other, and, on the other hand, a melt-digging integration, where a multicultural society emerges from several cultures that eventually develop a new common culture, but with more shades than you had before. Thirdly, we have segregation, which involves the compulsory or voluntary separation of minority groups from the majority population, most often expressed by the systematic division of housing lines, but also by its own parallel institutions in addition to religious communities, which in the host country are entrusted with public authority, for example. a truly binding decision for the individual on family matters. Assimilation and segregation are opposites to integration. Assimilation means unilateral adaptation on the part of immigrants, and it does not provide much room for diverging ways of living. Segregation means permanent parallel societies. In my opinion, integration is the answer to what assimilation and segregation represent of problems. Successful integration respects different cultural practices, but also requires mutual commitment. Integration occurs on several levels. Firstly, it is a system integration that refers to the state – and in and of the municipality – and the policies of the supporting institutions towards ethnic minorities, both in terms of the allocation of civil, social and political rights, and in terms of the economic and social position of the minority in society. The integration message that we are dealing with today is written primarily for this purpose of system integration. But there's another level that is equally important. Social integration underlines the opinion of the majority and minorities of attitudes to and behaviour towards each other, both collectively and not least as individuals – that is, with the attitudes and practices we meet with each other in everyday life: at kindergartens, at school, at work, in leisure and outdoors. Although, from the perspective of individuals, it may be difficult to distinguish between the two integration pathways, there may be grounds for distinguishing between system integration and social integration. One can easily imagine a number of situations in which ethnic minorities are fully system-integrated, but remain socially unintegrated. Even I have found inspiration in the supplementary culture model of social psychologist John Berry. According to this, as individuals, we can have more cultural positions, where integration also means keeping to our cultural base and our cultural practices, but at the same time being open and positive to and influenced by the majority culture in which we live. I hope we're on our way to the last one. Integration is slower than the optimists imagined, but what is most important is that it goes much better than the pessimistics have predicted. Although the distinction between immigration policy and integration policy has been practical in many respects, there is also a link between integration and immigration. In a democracy, the perception of how successful we are with integration will affect people's attitudes about how tight the door to Norway is to be. As an example of this, I refer to the previous article. The public integration policy, which we are dealing with in this position, has brought together a broad majority in the position. There's a positive basic attitude that those who come to realize their own goals, and as part of this, will participate in society and develop it economically, can be involved. At the same time, it is a realistic approach. You have to make demands on the immigrants as to ourselves. Passive help is not enough. Whether you have lived for a short time or a long time, you have to be a performing and participating member of society – on an equal footing.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-21.ana.ud1877e653	In this paper, the government is examining principles and frameworks for future integration policies. Lone minors have a higher level of housing, but that's because this group is a priority. Another measure that I want to draw up is the government's desire to assess the right to an introduction programme for refugees who find themselves self-inhabitable. The government believes that this measure may help to facilitate the settlement work outside the municipality and remedy the situation pointed out by the National Audit Office. It is also problematic that this measure could lead to a strong concentration of applicants for refugees in some cities, and it is therefore assessed whether this measure should only apply to places that are not typical areas of pressure. The Communication emphasises that there is a strong desire among immigrants to live urbanly, yet there are many district municipalities that live in many refugees. Several investigations point to many positive aspects of the settlement of refugees in the municipality of Norway. The report mentions, among other things: smaller communities make it easier to engage and settle refugees in local organisational life. Furthermore, figures show that immigration and labour migration account for about 70 pst. of population growth in the country. Several small coastal communities are therefore experiencing growth in the population because of this, and it is positive and desirable. My home municipality, with a little over 6,000 inhabitants, has a desire to be a municipality that settles for refugees. At the request of IMDi, the municipality has decided to maintain the 2012 level of residence also for 2013. This means that in 2013, this small municipality will seek to settle for 90 new refugees. I believe that the message illustrates some of the work done in Vadsø Municipality, namely that this is about facilitating people to have a place, and it is about facilitating that we in Vadsø Municipality can also participate with our solidarity – locally, as well as internationally.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-21.ana.ud1877e710	In the last 30 years, Norway has experienced increased immigration, and we have become a more diverse country. Immigration brings with it new cultures, traditions, once unknown religions, but also new challenges. Increased immigration into the country has also had an impact on the fight against violence in close relations. I am pleased that the Storing Message also addresses this challenge. It is important to remember that women with a migrant and refugee background are a complex group. They come from different societies, with different social practices. The life situation and position of these women in society can make them extra vulnerable. Their opportunities for language training and access to housing, work, social security rights and relief services may be lower than those of women in the majority population. The risk of violence continuing increases in the absence of social inclusion and, in the worst case, isolation, without others being able to stop it. Some men from minority families want to keep the woman in the family isolated to maintain or gain power and control. This may include, inter alia: means refusing to attend Norwegian courses or threatening that she will be sent out of the country. Thus, the woman is deprived of the opportunity to establish a social network and create a basis for independent living. We know that the majority of those currently using the housing services of crisis centres are women with a migrant and refugee background. In 2001, women with a migrant background accounted for one third of users, and in 2011 62 pst. by users of women with ethnic minority backgrounds. But about a quarter of the women were subjected to violence from an ethnic Norwegian partner. The increase in the proportion of women with a migrant background in crisis centres does not mean that there is more violence in families with a migrant background. An explanation may be that an increasing proportion of women exposed to violence in the majority population have their own income, good support from their own social network and increased knowledge of their rights. This makes it easier for them to leave a violent partner at an earlier time than before, while at the same time being able to use accommodation other than the crisis centres. The crisis centre offer is particularly important for violent women without labour income who have weak social networks where they cannot expect support in the event of a breach. This will in many cases be the situation for violent women with a migrant background. Women are particularly vulnerable to violence and abuse both before, during and after flight from war or conflict situations. Women living in reception centres may therefore be in a particularly vulnerable situation. Being a refugee can have consequences for physical and mental health. In addition to pre- and pre-flight traumas, loss of family, networks, status, identity and changes in relationships are common consequences. Refugees are therefore a high-risk group for the development of mental and social problems as well as other health problems. Exile can lead to increased family violence, and violence can increase to an extent in a situation of reception. Men with a minority background also experience violence in close relationships. In 2011, 48 out of 79 men visited the crisis centre had foreign origin. The reason they went to the crisis centres was coercion, child abuse, rape, sexual violence, trafficking in human beings, the danger of kidnapping in relation to children, honor-related violence and the fear of detention. Some immigrants, especially refugees, have worse living conditions than the rest of the population, and some experience violence. Fortunately, we see a improvement in living conditions after they have lived in Norway for a while, and there is considerable social mobility when we look at Norwegian-born immigrants’ parents. Nevertheless, immigrant groups have problems getting a good connection with the labour market and remain in the low-income group. This applies in particular to refugee families with many children where their parents are weak or have no occupational connection. These are challenges that we need to take seriously, and meet with different measures aimed at the individual family and individual. I have now listed specific challenges facing people with a minority background. However, I will end by saying the following: Those I have spoken about today are strong. Those who leave a violent husband or violent parent to start a new life often leave far more than a husband or close family. They often leave their entire social network. They go even if they risk standing alone, often without a job. It requires courage and will. I want from this pulpit to say to those who have the courage to seek help to go and create a new life: I have enormous respect for you.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-21.ana.ud1877e808	The challenges posed by the health situation of immigrants are widespread. The immigrant population in Norway is not a homogeneous group. These are different people who have come to Norway for different reasons: refugees, asylum seekers, migrant workers and family reunification. This also presents some challenges for healthcare, which we need to take seriously. Report. St. 6 for 2012–2013 ‘A comprehensive policy of integration – diversity and community’ was presented in autumn 2012. It is an important message and a key document for those who have come here from other countries, for society and for all of us who are engaged in a society that offers equal opportunities and an equivalent healthcare service. The five largest immigrant groups in Norway today are from Poland, Sweden, Pakistan, Somalia and Iraq. Most immigrants come from EEA countries and European countries outside the EU. 44 pst. by all immigrants come from Asia, Africa and South and Central America. There is good knowledge of the health situation among some long-term immigrant groups in Norway. We have more lack of knowledge about immigrants with shorter living time. In particular, this applies to immigrants from western countries, although they account for more than half of the immigrant population. A 2005/2006 study on health and living conditions among groups of immigrants showed that there is an increased incidence of type 2 diabetes and a higher incidence of mental illness among some of the investigated groups. In some immigrant groups, there are also significant gender differences related to health. Such findings are important to take seriously health care and try to do something about them. Real access to health and care services is important. The legal right to health care is not equivalent to real access to health care services. There are many different barriers, such as: economic, organisational and linguistic, which may restrict real access to health and care services. The quality of services will also affect treatment outcomes and how people can benefit from health and care services. Migration processes can often involve mental stress. Events — before, during and after migration — may be stressful and in some cases traumatic. Each person’s experience is individual experience. Migration poses challenges in the meeting with a new society, new language, new climate, new lifestyle and new standards. How people experience and master this varies. Therefore, there are wide variations between individuals and groups in the impact of migration on health. There may be many reasons why patients and users with a migrant background do not receive or experience receiving equivalent health and care services. This may lead to the patient and the user experiencing discrimination and discrimination — a problem that this message is also about. I want to mention some points that show what the government has done and will do in this area. It will: contribute to equivalent health and care services, including: By providing better-organised information, a communication on public health to the EEA in spring 2013, including: will have a broad coverage of the specific health challenges of immigrants and, in the context of the message, a national strategy on immigrant health will continue in 2013 the public health projects in Groruddalsssetting, which target the immigrant population to a large extent that grants to the NAKMI, the national competence unit for minority health will be further strengthened, and the current year will have a total of 8 million operational funds. Start up a separate initiative to qualify more people with a migrant background for vocational training in the field of health improving the knowledge base on the health of immigrants and their use of health services have better organised online services for immigrants. We have many health challenges – including for the immigrant population. But people who have come to Norway also have cultural health benefits. Many people have a healthier diet, with lots of fruit and vegetables, fewer use tobacco or drugs. This is positive, from a public health point of view. I would also like to underline how migrant workers do an invaluable and amazing job in the care, health and care sector. It also belongs to the picture when we talk about health and integration. Finally, living in a diverse society is a positive challenge – also for our health – by meeting people who represent something other than what we already know. I think that's good for all of us in this country.

It's a difficult challenge, and it's been about classes. Here classes evolve – here it develops really classes! The red-green government should focus more on how these classes evolve. When there is a large labour force benefit in a market with low professional rights, we know what the consequences are. A debate on ideology is therefore necessary: the free movement of labour and capital. It is an ideology that needs to be corrected. It will not be able to function over time; it will need more governance, because it is the nation states that will ultimately be responsible for the well-being and well-being of citizens, workers and asylum seekers. This will require the necessary instruments to ensure the extension of the jealous economic conditions peculiar to Norway, which are an important part of our own kind.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-21.ana.ud1877e1085	When we have just had a long and good debate on the importance of a targeted and comprehensive integration policy, with a focus on diversity, opportunities and communities, I find that the issue we are about to deal with is in quite a contrast to that. We are now going to take a position on a proposal for a representative that goes in the opposite direction. The proposal has been made by representatives Ørsal Johansen, Hagesetter and Starheim, all from the Progress Party, and is about tightening up the requirements for permanent residence permits in Norway. Some people will probably think that the requirements are too strict as they are. The majority of the population believe that the requirements should lie where they are and do not see the need to amend the legislation, at least not on the basis of this proposal. Nor did the Committee consider that it was necessary to consult on this matter. The proposers are promoting a total of ten proposals which are intended to make it more difficult for people to stay permanently. At the same time, the same proposers say that the aim is for those moving to Norway to be able to support themselves as quickly as possible. The last thing we all agree on, but none of the other parties here at Stortinget supports a view that is that making it harder to stay permanently is a good and sensible contribution to achieving that goal. The proposals promoted by the Progress Party in this matter can be divided into three groups. Six of the proposals deal with different ways of extending the period needed for permanent residence. Two of the proposals reinforce the requirements beyond that, while two proposals are to be regarded as a form of reward for so-called good behaviour. The period before permanent residence may be granted shall be doubled from today's requirement for three years' residence to a requirement of six years, cf. proposal No I. In addition, proposal No. 4 with a claim to have worked full-time for two and a half of the last three years and may not have received daily or social benefits for more than six months in the last three years. Proposal No. 6 implies that everyone must have passed a test in Norwegian before permanent residence can be granted. Proposals Nos. 9 and 10 means a significant tightening of the possibilities for family reunification by requiring the referee to have permanent residence first and that family reunification should never be granted the right to further family reunification. In addition, proposal No. 5 that all must make a declaration of integration and active citizenship before permanent residence can be granted. All the other parties oppose these proposals. A three-year residence should be more than enough. Nor do we support the requirement to work for a certain period. There are family immigration demands, and the majority believe that this is enough. As regards the question of passing a test in Norwegian for permanent residence, the majority point out that there is a discussion of this in the case of citizenship and that the discussion of language requirements should be limited to it. The proposals for further restrictions on the possibilities of family immigration are partly contrary to the European Convention on Human Rights and will, on the one hand, constitute a severe restriction on the ability of children to reunite with a parent in Norway. In the majority's view, these are totally unreasonable demands. In addition, the Progress Party wants to further tighten the rules we already have to be able to refuse residence and deport persons who commit criminal acts. The majority believe the current legislation is good enough. Refusal of residence for all persons dependent on some form of financial benefit is also not reasonable, as the majority sees it. Finally, a few proposals are promoted which can be said to be a form of premiere of what the Progress Party considers to be good behaviour. The requirement for residence may be reduced by voluntary activity. The majority agree that such activities themselves are positive and can contribute to good integration, but it is difficult to see that it serves neither organisations nor individual members to introduce an element of coercion as initially voluntary activity. Finally, the majority also do not support a system where it takes time for permanent residence, depending on the speed with which individual is able to implement the introduction programme. It can quickly become some sort of unfair sorting. The majority are voting against all ten proposals, which the Progress Party is going to make on its own.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-21.ana.ud1877e1108	Some of the backgrounds for promoting this proposal for a representative are growing concerns about immigration to an extent that allows the Nordics to become a minority in their own country in the long term. Norway is not the country in Europe that receives the most immigrants, but Norway is the country that receives the most in relation to its population. Current immigration policy, for which the government is responsible and supported by the opposition parties – with the exception of the Progress Party – is neither responsible nor economically sustainable. As I see it, the current immigration policy is based on two myths. One myth is that immigration pays off. This is demonstrably wrong. On 10 May 2011 there was an important signal. It was the day that the Brochmann Committee delivered its nomination, NOU 2011: 7, Welfare and Migration, the future of the Norwegian model. The conclusion of the NOU is that immigration is not economically sustainable. If anyone in this room against assumption has not read the NOU and wonder why, I can tell you that the professional participation of immigrants is significantly lower than that of comparable Norwegians. Since then, this has been confirmed by the SSB. In the report ‘Macroeconomics and public finances in different scenarios for immigration’, presented in May 2012, one of the conclusions was that non-Western immigration weakens public finances. Furthermore, the report shows that from 2020 Norway will have a rather strenuous economy if nothing changes. The combination of high immigration and the world’s best welfare systems is a bomb under the welfare state. Continued high levels of immigration will mean that we will not be able to maintain our relatively generous welfare systems. It appears that the working life cycle of large immigrant groups is too short. The result is that two out of three immigrants go to social security, according to a report from the Frischsenter at the University of Oslo. 35 pst. the State budget is used for social security. It's a fainting 370 billion. kr. 20 pst. social security benefits for the pre-employment population. Can someone then tell me that it's sustainable? The second myth relates to multicultural diversity. What gain has this diversity given us? What we know is that the Grorud valley is being drained for the North, which, unlike most politicians, is experiencing diversity on a close basis. We know that Oslo is no longer a safe city, and that women should not go out alone at night. We also know that in countries where development has gone beyond this, the Heads of State, Angela Merkel, David Cameron, and now Nicholas Sarkozy have departed from multiculturalism. The biggest mistake that has been made in Norway in terms of integration is that one has not made demands on immigrants. Instead, we've tried to adapt. I find this completely pointless. What kind of guests make demands on the host? If one does not thrive in the host company, one can take advantage of one of the freedoms you have, namely to leave the company. The proposals we are making in this matter are, in our opinion, not unreasonable claims, but any claim that anyone wants to be part of the Norwegian community may seem affordable. Very familiar they haven't, but the Council of State has failed to answer the questions because she disagreed with the proposals, and didn't see the benefit of it.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-21.ana.ud1877e1188	Immigration legislation is constantly evolving, and several of the key changes that have been made in the last decade include: aimed at facilitating better integration. Intensifications have been made in the asylum policy towards persons who do not have a real need for protection, the family immigration legislation has been reinforced, and mandatory training in Norwegian and social skills has been required to obtain permanent residence permits. At the same time, we all agree that there are still challenges and the need to develop policy further. I believe, however, that the proposals that the rapporteurs have put forward here are not the right ones. I think it is necessary to remember what is the main consequence of a permanent residence permit, namely that you have the right to stay even when you no longer meet the conditions of the original residence permit. This means, for example: that if you were married, you do not lose your residence permit even if your marriage ends. If you extend the residence requirement to, for example: six years – as proposed by the proposers – this means that the alienation authorities have to regularly assess whether the conditions for the original authorisation are still met until six years. If the foreigner is not allowed to leave Norway. For example, the representatives consider that: that a foreign spouse who has lived for five years in Norway will have to leave the country if the marriage that gave the residence permit was broken? I have noticed that the proposal can be understood in such a way that the increased terms of the permanent residence permit will only be decisive for the status of the residence permit – whether it will be permanent or renewed every year. I am referring to the fact that the proposers in the vote say that the person who has come as a spouse through the family immigration code should not be refused further residence even if the marriage is dissolved, but that it will only take longer to obtain a permanent residence permit. In this case, it is unclear to me which renewal assessment the proposers believe should be carried out. I must stress that the introduction of such aggravated conditions as the proposers wish will result in a more complex and comprehensive procedure to determine who is entitled to a permanent residence permit. And in this context, I have to remind you that last year, the foreign administration dealt with more than 18 000 cases of permanent residence permits. I would also like to stress that the proposal will lead to a large number of people refusing to apply for a permanent residence permit. However, they will continue to comply with the terms of the temporary residence permit, and their cases must therefore be reviewed at each renewal – which I suppose the proposers believe will happen once a year. This will, of course, lead to increased use of resources in the foreign administration. When it comes to defining immigration policy, we must have an overall approach. It is in the context of initial permits that we need to give the most attention to control and consideration to regulated immigration. In the case of renewal and permanent residence permits, the main focus must be on rapid and effective treatment. Of course, in this context we should also pay attention to control and regulation, but on this point I believe that today's legislation maintains a good balance. As Minister of Justice and Defence, I can assure you that I am also committed to having the necessary rules to respond to foreigners who commit crime. The most important thing in this context is the rules on expulsion. In those cases where expulsion is not relevant, we have rules on the quarantine period for a permanent residence permit. I will always be aware that adjustments may be needed, but I do not think that is the basis for such horizontal changes proposed by the proposers.
