ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-21.ana.ud1877e1358	In connection with the proposal by the representatives of the big business representatives, Geir Jørgen Bekkevold, Hans Olav Syversen and Kjell Ingolf Ruppstad on better coordination and strategic management of ICT projects in the public sector, I would like to refer to the comments of the majority of the committee. The majority, consisting of the members of the Workers' Party, the SV and the Centre, support the assessments of individual proposals by the Council of State. In 2012, the Government presented the digitisation programme ‘Net with the population’, which will facilitate a faster meeting between the public sector and the citizens of Norway. It is also expected that such a change, or rotation, from traditional public meeting places to digitised meeting places, will improve public sector resource use. The majority of the Committee also emphasises the efforts of the government to bring together and establish a common technical platform and an adapted regulatory framework for the digital management of the country's common ICT solutions. Proposals challenge too wide a variation in national ICT solutions, highlighting the weakness of communication between systems. The government responds to this issue when, in dealing with this issue, it highlights the work of establishing a common infrastructure – a basis for all countries’ ICT solutions. The purpose of such a digital base wall is to create so-called common components, which can be used together and reused by all public enterprises. Examples of common components are platforms such as Altinn, the ID port, the People's Register, the Unit Register and the Matrikkel. The extent to which these platforms are used varies slightly, but the objective is for all public undertakings to put this infrastructure into service. The majority of the Committee expects that the coordinated digitisation work launched at ministerial level will contribute to good digital joint solutions. The majority of the Committee considers that the coordination and involvement of digitisation will provide a good framework for the operation and management of the public sector. Although coordination and re-use work is to a large extent, I believe that diversity in the public sector will sometimes also require specific sectorial ICT solutions. I believe it would be unrealistic to expect all services to be delivered on the same platform. This can also be cost-effective for the core activities of the agency, because it adapts the service to the system rather than vice versa. The government also highlights this issue in its letter to the Committee and therefore shows a very broad reflection on the issues raised in this matter. In conclusion, I would like to highlight some of the assumptions on which the minority in the Committee bases these proposals, namely the perception of a public sector that is a waste of public funds, which lacks or lacks capacity and understanding of rational activities, and that in the public sector there is a ‘generally weak understanding of the importance of equipment investment in order to support the efficiency of individual employees’. These are assumptions and generalisation of the public sector which the undersigned does not support. I think it's an attempt to paint a picture of a public sector that's totally inefficient, that doesn't do the things they're supposed to do, and that doesn't take responsibility for the tasks they're going to do, in terms of economics. I think that is wrong. If we use health care examples, where you work very rationally, and where you have a big focus on keeping costs in check and providing good service, then all the prerequisites for this proposal must give a wrong picture of the public sector. I'd like to highlight that.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-21.ana.ud1877e1404	Let me start with the positive. One of the country's most important ICT projects, Altinn, seems to have worked better this year than in previous years. It's worth it to rejoice over. With the fact that the case-rapporteur mentioned Altinn, who was one of the starting points of this proposal for a representative, I think it is right to mention it – and start with the positive. We're going to enjoy it. Nevertheless, the state's ICT management cannot be informed, in our view. The state does not have an overview of how much ICT is spent this year. Staff in Nav, in the police and in the health sector are concerned about systems that do not always work, and digital everyday life is still far away in many sectors. On the basis of these problems, there may be several structural challenges. Firstly, there is a lack of coordination and failed incentives for efficient resource use. Why, for example, should The state pension fund has its own digital pension calculator for the inhabitants, when Nav had already developed one? Why have several patient journal systems been introduced instead of having one? There is also a lack of coordination of the functional requirements of the ICT system. The contracts specify the characteristics of the ICT solution, but do not specify whether any requirements are more important than others. The result is that suppliers have to solve all the requirements, often making solutions unnecessarily complex, and increasing complexity increases the risk of errors and is considerably more resource-intensive. Today, the Christian People's Party is promoting proposals to bring together political and administrative responsibility for all major government ICT projects in one department. Such overall governance can improve the coordination of ICT projects, so as to avoid today's situation where large projects are run almost independently of each other, and where the same problem is usually solved several times. Better coordination of operational organisations and better interconnection of procurement and solutions may be possible, so as to avoid different state-owned enterprises choosing different solutions to meet the same needs. Secondly, better management of the individual project is needed, and competence building is important here. It is necessary to establish incentives for cost-effective solutions that are reasonable to manage. The Ministry responsible for ICT management should be part of the project organisations with an owner’s perspective and should be a driver for optimal use of the funds. Thirdly, dietary/benefit analysis is not enough in relation to public ICT projects. Thus, functionality is often implemented, even if, for example: manual handling would be more profitable. This makes the solutions unnecessary expensive and complex. The Christian People's Party proposes that more comprehensive requirements should be made for diet-benefit analyses to be carried out in order to limit the costs and complexity of projects. In addition, the Christian People's Party proposes a long-term overall ICT strategy for state-owned enterprises covering the following key elements: Firstly, it is appropriate to provide an overview of large state-owned ICT investments, almost as far as road development is concerned. Secondly, there should be leads for overall road choices, e.g.: common applications across government units and centralisation of databases and management. There are a number of databases with personal data that may be manageable in a more reasonable and safer way, such as: the public register, the Nav, the police and the licence register. Thirdly, assessments should be made of the potential for collapsing large projects with overlapping functional areas or using standardised tools rather than tailoring. Fourth, a State ICT strategy should include leadership for the implementation of projects, including: prioritisation of requirements, standardisation, dietary/benefit analyses, acquisition and profit implementation. These are some of the things that the Christian People's Party is addressing in this proposal for a representative, and I am pleased that both the Advance Party and the Right partly support both the intention and some of the proposals.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-21.ana.ud1877e1437	Investment in ICT is important but also difficult. They are important because they are key to an efficient and user-friendly public sector. The Committee points out in its opinion that the Castle and the Castle Square constitute one of the main symbols of Norway and the national community. The castle site is also a tourist attraction, which every year is visited by thousands of tourists from abroad. As the nation's foremost parade ground, the Castle Square will have a central role in marking the Constitutional Jubilee. It is a long-term rehabilitation work that is now under way, both for those who travel daily across the Castle Square, for tourism and for the nation as such. For too long we have had a castle site that has sometimes been a little representative, difficult to move on and that has caused problems for the neighbors around. The problems are linked to poor drainage. Large amounts of precipitation lead to a run-off of rainwater that excavates large runs, pits and holes. When the water runs down the Castle Square, it takes a lot of gravel. The condition has deteriorated from year to year and increases the cost of maintenance and repair. Neighbors have not been modest either. It is reported that the Royal Court has received complaints from the University of Oslo, which has suffered water damage in the historical library, because of gravel that has stopped to drain water. Rehabilitation of the Castle Square has been discussed several times in recent years. Top-level coverage selection has been a topic for discussion. The solution to which it is now added implies the establishment of a new fixed top of stabilised gravel in a transparent binder. Well, as important as the new deck is the basic work to be done, with the replacement of mass and drainage. The project also involves the rehabilitation of roads and pavements related to the site. All of this will give the Slottsplatz a representative look and a more rational operating and maintenance situation. A cost framework of 70 million is proposed. kr. The project is proposed in the Proposition funded by Staatsbygg kap. 1581 Royal property rights item 30 Larger rehabilitation projects. For 2013 it is granted 37.94 million. kr in this entry for the completion of the roof project at the Royal Palace of Stockholm. However, the final forecast of the project is about. Twenty million. DKR below the budget. Twenty million. Therefore, re-disposal of kr to the project at Slottspladsen is proposed. In addition, it is proposed to increase the allocation of entry 30 by 37.5 million. kr and to increase item 1 Operating expenditure by 2,5 million. kr. As indicated in the opinion, the Committee took note of the fact that the text of the proposal was not fully consistent with the proposal for a decision. The non-compliance concerns the proposal to increase the capped allocation. 1581 Royal property rights item 30 Larger rehabilitation projects. The text of the proposal refers to an increase of 75.44 million. kr, whereas the draft decision contains 74,44 million. kr. The Committee considers that the amount resulting from the proposal for a decision in the proposal appears to be an obvious incurrency and therefore proposes that the decision to amend the budget be taken under the heading. 1581 item 30 represents an increase to 75.44 million. kr – not to SEK 74,44 million. kr as stated in the draft decision.

Increased municipal autonomy presupposes that municipalities assume their responsibility for safeguarding important natural and outdoor interests. Over the period 2000–2005, a pilot project with expanded local autonomy was carried out in eight selected municipalities. The municipalities that have been involved in the project have been allowed to establish both tourist loops and recreational loops. The experiment has been ex-post evaluated by the Norwegian Institute for Natural Research on behalf of the Ministry of Environment. The evaluation shows that all six intermediate targets for better motor traffic were fully or partially met in the experimental municipalities. In fact, the population of municipalities without snowmobile loops was more plagued by noise than the population of municipalities participating in the test scheme. The seven municipalities that already have experiments with these loops are part of the 40. There will be an application scheme to host among the 40 municipalities to be included in the scheme. Clear conditions and frameworks for the establishment of such pathways shall apply in the municipalities. Among other things, it is forbidden to lay loops in some areas, such as: the protection area, the wild cleaning area or the essential open-air area. Municipalities bordering Finland and Sweden will be able to establish supply loops for the loop network there. In municipalities in Troms and Finnmark, where pleasure runs in established loops are already allowed, it will be easier to connect loops across local boundaries and increase the possibility of running from travel life facilities to the loop network. I intend to send the suggestions shortly after Easter. The details of the proposals will then be returned to the department in connection with the hearing letter. On the basis of this, I also believe it is right to vote against the proposals for representatives that open up both snowmobile running and bar-field running in general, and that we have found a good and balanced solution that will be presented later in more detail in the hearing letter. I think we have found a solution where we take care of both nature and outdoor life, while at the same time increasing the possibility of pleasure running with snowmobiles.

Access to the project is granted upon application to municipalities within the territorial policy scope. A scheme is being set up for the period 2014–2018 to allow 40 municipalities to test out recreational activities on foot. Municipalities forming part of the scheme may decide to set up loops through land-use planning in accordance with the rules of the Planning and Building Act. ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-21.ana.ud1877e2420	The size shall now address a proposal aimed at maintaining Statnet’s User Council, the issue of drawing up a national plan for power lines and costs in the central network. The proposals are supported by a majority in the Committee, and I would like to state that the majority of the Storting – the three governmental parties – are currently in a minority of the Committee. The majority have concluded that Statnett’s User Council and users’ representation on Statnett SF’s Board of Directors will be maintained. The majority also ask the Government to draw up a national plan for power lines, which is updated and promoted for treatment in the Storting once every major period. The majority are also in favour of the government presenting during the spring session 2013 a case on the allocation of costs in the central network between producers and consumers. I assume that the majority of the committee itself will explain in more detail its views. I would like to draw attention to the fact that the Stortinget decided, as late as possible in the treatment of the current state budget, that the system of a separate advice by central network users should cease. In the 2013 budget paper, it was noted that, in the light of the major development tasks of Statnet over the next few years, it is important to ensure the neutrality of the enterprise. It is important to maintain this change, because this must be seen in the context of the major changes in the power supply that took place during the reorganisation of State power. The involvement of users in the undertaking should be organised in future in a way that is more consistent with the ordinary organisation of companies. As regards the need for a national plan for power lines, the government parties do not share this view. A Joint Committee agrees that it is Statnet and the major regional network operators responsible for the construction and operation of the central power grid and that the energy authorities have a number of measures and instruments that affect the investments. The Committee points out that external quality assurance requirements for large liner projects are in place and that this strengthens the control of energy authorities by the choice of the concept and the professional quality of the decision-making basis. On the part of the government parties, we see this process as reassuring and it facilitates the necessary net investments. This is also the best basis for ensuring a secure power supply for the whole country. I don't think it's hard to understand the industry's concern about what this will mean for them. At the same time, I know that we have a government that is concerned about the survival of the industry, and that we need to create a long-term perspective for the companies. This is well documented by the grips that have been taken so far for the industry. That is why I feel confident that this will also be the result in this case. I even come from a part of the country that hurt needs a network that is much more time-consuming than today, and that can provide security for the power supply that is so vital for both housing and business. By letter to the Committee, the Council of State has also presented in a very good manner the cost allocation of the central network and an overall committee shares the government's view that the costs of the masked central network should continue to be allocated to all customers. Government parties also share the view of the Council of State that, as a system administrator, Statnett has the best chance of seeing the development of foreign connections towards the Norwegian power system as a whole. Government parties cannot therefore see the need to promote a separate case for the Storting on the allocation of costs in the central network. I then take up the present proposal from the Labour Party, the Socialist Left Party and the Central Party.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-21.ana.ud1877e2453	Then the representative Bendiks H. Arnesen took the proposal to which he referred.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-21.ana.ud1877e2456	The proposed representative 8:17 S for 2012–2013 on user representation in the bodies of Statnett; a national plan for power lines and cost allocation in the central network is closely linked to the proposed representative 8:18 S for 2012–2013 on a future-oriented network structure. However, the Committee wanted and chose to examine these proposals separately. Since the early 1990s, little has been invested in the central transmission system. On the other hand, there has been a need to improve efficiency, and there is today a great need to increase capacity and rebuild parts of the mains. It is necessary to expand the mains power supply for several reasons. The most important thing is to provide better security for electricity supplies to parts of the country that currently have a weak central network. Renewable energy has been given a real driver of the scheme of green certificates together with Sweden. It creates the need for new power lines to bring the power to the consumer. The plans for electrification of the socket also contribute to creating demand for new power lines. The central network is a national infrastructure that benefits the whole country. It is in the interest of most people to maintain and develop this infrastructure. The costs are allocated to all customers in the central network. New consumption or production is often linked to the central network via radials. The use of radials can usually be traced back to a few users. The State considers that it is economically appropriate that a small number cover all the costs of these radials. Construction contributions and tariff provisions may become excessive. The Progress Party considers that it is appropriate to consider whether large-scale society will also benefit greatly from the radials and thereby take part in the costs. Statnett has very large development plans for the next decade. The last revised central network plans have a cost framework of between 50 and 70 billion. kr. Only these investments may lead to a doubling of the current network tariff for all users of the network. In addition, investments to the regional and distribution network are estimated to be 40 to 50 billion. kr. The power grid is a monopoly, and today's system is that all costs are borne by consumers. The party to the progress is of the opinion that part of the costs should be covered by the Community above the tax return. If it is done this way, it will help to get more industry going in the districts. Today, this is difficult, among other things. due to expensive power and network costs. To date, the largest users of the central network have been represented on Statnett’s board. This scheme was terminated following a decision in the State budget. This made Statnet’s bodies less visible, and the main players in this market believe that their views are so important that the system of user councils should not have been terminated. The proposers of the proposal are of the clear opinion that the system of Statnet's User Council and user representation on the Statnet Board should be continued. The proposers believe that it would be natural for Stortinget to deal with the planned investments in the central network. Such treatment would be in line with Storting’s treatment of the petroleum sector and investments in trade. The proposers consider it a crossroads to plan billions of investments in the central network without the Stortinge to deal with the matter. What is desirable is that central network plans are subject to the treatment of the Stortinge during each major period. The proposers believe that the large costs of the central network should be subject to a closer review of the Stortinget. A lot of the costs are related to non-residential dwellings. The revenues from these cables will largely benefit the power producers. The discount should therefore assess the network charge. The Progress Party believes that some of these costs could be covered by the State. This would be similar to the fact that the State directly covers part of the costs of other infrastructure without full coverage by users. Finally, I would recommend the Committee's position, which is supported by the Advancement Party, the Right and the Christian People's Party.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-21.ana.ud1877e2479	Statnet has large investment plans for the central network over the next decade, and in the revised network development plan presented in July 2012, the investment need is estimated to be in the order of 50 to 70 billion. kr. There is a strong political consensus about the need for investment – both in order to remove bottlenecks in the network, in order to receive increased amounts of renewable energy in line with the objectives of the electricity certificate market and the EU renewable directive, and in terms of security of supply and to ensure preparedness for a society that is increasingly electrified and where we rely on electricity for vital societal functions. Major investments also mean changes in network tariffs. Statnett is a monopolistic, and it's the users of the network – i.e. households, businesses, industry and power producers – who pay after a specified key. Right-wing believes it would be natural to discuss the cost allocation of the central network in the light of the large investments we have ahead of us. We have called for this assessment in several contexts, including: Last year the Stortinget processed the online message ‘We are building Norway – on the deployment of the mains power supply’. The Labour Party, the Centre Party and the Socialist Left have not wanted to take this debate and assessment – despite signals and fear of the consequences of a two to threefold increase in tariffs for industry. Here we are talking about large sums – several tens of millions in increased costs. Industry requires the central network tariff to take account of the competitive situation of industry. Many actors currently have a rather marginal profitability. Unfortunately, 250 employees at Södra Cell at Tofte got a heavy message yesterday – without me linking this directly to future changes in the central network tariff, but used as an example that many industrial companies are in a rather demanding situation. Power producers, for their part, also fear an increase in tariffs and point out that increased costs may lead to a reduction in investment in Norway during the electricity licence market. If this is true, we risk that Norwegian consumers will subsidise investments in renewable energy in Sweden, since Norway will in any case fund half of the 26.4 TWh new renewable production of the electricity certificate market to stimulate the development of Norway and Sweden. So in this picture, the government is choosing to continue today's network cost distribution. State networks are currently working on a revised central network tariff strategy for the period 2014-2018. This is at best an underestimation of the impact of the large network investments in the years to come, and at worst a discussion of the government and the red-green parties do not dare. A monopoly such as Statnett depends on the trust of both authorities, politicians and customers – i.e. households, industry and other business. This means that Statnet must strive to maximise transparency and good dialogue around its priorities and activities. Furthermore, Statnet must be able to deliver projects within time and cost frameworks that demonstrate control at all stages. Transparency and transparency could also be a quality assurance for Statnet. On the right, the government believes that a wrong decision was made by placing Statnet's User Council and the users' representation on Statnet's board of directors. We need more, no less transparency and transparency in a period of so much investment. This representative proposal, which we have promoted together with the Progress Party, the Christian People's Party and the Left, also deals with a desire for the Stortinget – at least once during a growing period – to be presented with a plan for central network investments in the future. The development of power lines can be controversial, and it can be an advantage of a more direct political anchoring in addition to the fact that we spend large amounts on network infrastructure without involving the Storting – unlike in some other areas, such as transport. We are moving towards a situation of surplus power in Norway. It is important to strengthen foreign exchange capacity – in order to increase security of supply in Norway, to increase value creation and to help our neighbouring countries achieve their climate objectives by delivering a balance of power to the continent. On the right, we believe that we need to increase ambitions in this area in terms of increased capacity, release all good forces in the construction of these links and signal an active attitude to cooperation with our neighbouring countries.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-21.ana.ud1877e2730	Today we are debating a forward-looking network structure for the transport of electricity from the producer to the consumer. While electricity is produced by many producers, the network companies across the country have a monopoly on their products. In order to have a secure power supply throughout the country that is both robust and of sufficient capacity, the network structure at all levels must be satisfactory. The electricity network in Norway is divided into three levels: distribution networks, regional networks and central networks. The responsible for the construction and operation of all mains networks is Statnett and the local network operators on site. The central network combines production and consumption in different parts of the country, provides access to the market for producers in all parts of the country and provides exchanges of electricity to all regions. The central network also includes international connections. Statnett owns approx. 90 pst. by the central network, while the remaining part is owned by the main electricity producers. The regional network connects the central network and the distribution network. The power grid is a natural monopoly. Its task is to transfer electrical current into its geographical area. Since all network activities are monopolistic, it is important that these activities are highly regulated and the responsibility is shared between energy authorities, system operators and transmission system operators. Network users finance the operation of the network and investments through the network lease. The network lease is for many a large part of the total electricity bill. Consumers cannot switch network providers, as they can with power suppliers. Therefore, network activities need to be monitored in order to operate optimally both economically and safely. The promotion party refers to the importance of putting the customer in the centre. Norway is facing major tasks of upgrading and deployment of transmission systems over the next decade. Statnett’s plans, which were last revised in 2012, range from 50 to 70 billion. kr in the next decade. In addition, these large investments will trigger upgrades on regional and distribution networks and new installations estimated at 40 to 55 billion. kr. At the top of this, investments in automatic metering systems, AMS, can amount to between 5 and 10 billion. kr. All these investments must be borne by consumers and not least by the power-generating industry around the country. Norway is a large country geographically, but with low population density outside the major cities and agglomerations. This costs a lot to build and operate a secure power grid in all three areas. The investments previously referred to shall all be covered by consumers and industry, in particular the power-generating industry. The monopoly activities, to which the mains power source is subject in Norway, must be subject to the strictest controls. Efficiency, planning and controlled deployment must be the pillars of any network development and rehabilitation project. Close control is required, but in many cases it is contradictory to the dividend policy expected from the owners, which is essentially the state and municipality. From several networks and power producers it is reported that companies have to borrow in order to pay expected dividends to owners. The Progress Party is concerned about this development, as the final bill for network costs is flipped over to consumers and industry. Today there are approximately 150 broadcasters – the smallest with only a few thousand subscribers. The Party considers that a significant reduction in the number of network carriers is necessary in order to strengthen operation and reduce unit costs. Pareto has shown that the unit costs are highest for the smallest companies. The Progress Party, together with the Right, proposes that the Stortinget ask the government during the Spring 2013 session to promote a case on regional and local networks for power transmission. This matter must be addressed in proposals that can provide an efficient and forward-looking network structure in Norway, with fewer and more robust network companies than today. What is real is that we tabled this proposal for a representative at the end of October 2012. So if you wanted it from the government, they'd probably be able to get a document that we're asking for. Finally, I would like to address the joint motion by the Committee on the Rules of Procedure, the Verification of Credentials and Immunities.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-21.ana.ud1877e2756	The representative Henning Skumsvoll has taken up the proposal he referred to.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-21.ana.ud1877e2759	As we also discussed in the previous case, we have to calculate large investments in the grid in the future. This applies both in the central network and in the underlying networks. A total investment framework of between 90 and 125 billion. kr for the next decade is likely to be based on Statnett’s latest network development plan and estimates provided by Norway’s Watercourse and Energy Directorate. Investments are deemed necessary. A modern society depends on power supply and security of supply for most functions. We need more and a stronger network to be able to phase in new amounts of energy – in line with both the electricity certificate agreement with Sweden and Norway’s renewable targets as part of the EU renewable directive. Furthermore, investment is deemed necessary in view of future expectations of a warmer, wilder and wetter climate, which will require a more robust infrastructure. The network activity is a monopoly, and that means that we, i.e. both politicians, public authorities, power and network companies have an extra heavy responsibility to protect their customers’ interests. Large investments in the regional and distribution networks in the future will mean increased network tariffs for customers, both private households, industry and other industrial activities. The right is concerned that customers will receive a good quality product at the most cost-effective price possible. Societal use of electricity in increasingly new areas, but we also use it more effectively, for example. at households. New construction requirements and different technology developments also mean that household power consumption is likely to decline. The network tariff will also appear to be more expensive than the electricity consumption itself, and some customers already experience this today and question why tariffs are so high. For the right, it is important that customers have a secure assurance that the electricity has been delivered in the most cost-effective way possible. In Norway, we have many online companies. Several reports suggest that a structural change in the direction of fewer and larger network carriers will be useful for customers and provide a socio-economic benefit both in terms of the cost picture and in order to ensure a good quality of infrastructure and services linked to the electricity network. The fact that there has not been any particularly significant changes in the network structure in the last decade, in combination with the large investments we are facing, is the background that the Right, together with the Progress Party and the Left, has promoted the proposal for a representative asking the Government to promote measures that can provide us with a more efficient and forward-looking regional and local network structure. Following the promotion of this proposal for a representative, the NVE has intervened in order to allow network operators that choose to merge to retain the gain of the merger for a longer period than in the past. This is good, and it's a step in the right direction. The question is whether this measure is enough. The financial framework under which network carriers operate is regulated and monitored. It is through these frameworks that we need to make changes that stimulate fewer and larger network companies. The NVE has notified further changes, and I think that is necessary. And it's urgent. Investment needs in the future will require a great deal of resources, both in terms of competence and capital. Furthermore, there are some changes in the value of the outputs along the value chain between power producers via network suppliers to final customers. Analyses presented at the Energy Norway Winter Conference last week, among others. by the consulting company McKinsey, shows how an increasing share of the values associated with power generation is being taken out more closely to the final customer. There is a decline in profitability upstream and a rise in profitability downstream and traditional network operations. How are the power companies to deal with the future margin pressure? This is both a challenge and an opportunity for power generation owners in Norway, i.e. mainly public owners such as municipalities, county councils and state. In order to participate in this value chain, it is important that network operators are able to offer good technology platforms and products and services to the final customer – and this requires both a sense of trend, competence, capital, capacity to implement and not least a desire to invest. If the network and power companies do not make use of this potential picture, there is a relatively high probability that other actors will take an increasing share of the value creation and profitability of the industry. There has been a great deal of uncertainty about the roll-out of the AMS and the requirement for when to implement it. I think it is good that the Council of State has chosen to postpone the deadline by a few years. It is important that we put in place technologies and solutions that are good and forward-looking. We are also pleased that the Council of State has chosen to close the plans and work for a common tariff for the central and regional networks.

The best Norwegian university is now in ninth place in the Nordic region, and the distance from the top is larger than before. Here we will find, among other things: universities in Lund, Uppsala and Helsinki in the places behind Karolinska. I'm aware that this kind of ranking can be problematic, that they hardly tell the whole truth, and that in individual cases they might give a direct wrong impression. But when major changes take place in a short time, such as: in the case of the University of Bergen, there may be reason to ask why. It is likely that it makes sense not to over-interpret results in individual surveys and rankings. But I will completely reject that the rankings that exist, and especially the Times Higher Education, should have no value or relevance whatsoever. There are some people in the academy in Norway who claim this, and I think that is a completely uninteresting debate. When rankings are based on a number of objective criteria, in this case for Times Higher Education 13 criteria, and they show consistent results over time, it's of great interest what they say. As we know, the international rankings are given more and more emphasis by researchers, students and businesses, all of whom make continuous assessments of the global knowledge landscape. In addition, the focus on rankings can become self-enhancing by directing resources and talents towards those ranked highest and thus further strengthening their position. It is therefore probably wiser to deal with the rankings than to deny them. In addition, the EU Education Commissioner has taken the initiative of a new and probably more comprehensive European ranking of universities, called U-Multirank, which will allow university environments to ‘benchmark’ themselves against the equivalent in other countries and at other institutions. This can be an interesting tool in raising world-class research environments, as both the Research Communication opens up and the Right is concerned about. But let me point out for sure: it's obviously not the location or ranking in itself I'm concerned about, but what it says about the quality and attractiveness of an institution internationally. Then, of course, the value of the profiling a given ranking could have for the relevant university and country. Like it or not, we're participating in an international knowledge race that might have more significance for the future than the arms race we saw during the cold war. So let me return to my starting point two years ago: what level of ambition should we have for our leading universities? What will that require, for example? for a Norwegian university to be ranked as the best in the Nordic region? Is that even possible? ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-21.ana.ud1877e2997	It is a fact that Norwegian universities come out worse on international rankings than the Danish and Swedish ones. The Nordic top institutions claim very well in competition, but they do not say they are satisfied with it. There may be many objections to the rankings. The research indicator is strongly emphasised, the data base is weak and the methodology is controversial. This results in the favour of research-intensive institutions at the expense of those who take a major responsibility for education, and that locations can vary considerably from one year to another, which the interpellant was also involved in. The universities and universities of Norway have a broad mandate based on three pillars: research, education and dissemination. Institutions must first and foremost be measured against how they carry out their broad and societal tasks through high quality in all these three areas. They shall ensure that Norway contributes to the global knowledge pool and shall, by being an attractive international partner, take back knowledge generated in other countries. They will contribute to an increased understanding of nature and of ourselves as human beings and as societies. Institutions are important for industrial development and for the quality of public services. Not least, institutions shall train candidates who may provide the necessary renewal for decades to come. The training must be based on both research and development and at the same time be relevant to the work to be undertaken by the candidates. However, we must recognise that the rankings indicate quality. The rankings often have in common that they are based on indicators recognised by the research community as a sign of quality. Other types of evaluations and measurements, such as fascination, publication and citation analyses and the impact of the European Research Council, show the same thing. The progress of Norwegian research is good, but there is more to go on. What can we expect from Norwegian universities in terms of international visibility, impact and quality? I welcome this invitation from the representative of Warloe to debate quality at Norwegian universities. Warloe is asking for strategic assessments of the level of ambition we should have for universities. As Warloe has probably registered, this is a theme the government is discussing in the recently submitted research report. It is obvious that Norway, which is the size of a small American state, cannot dominate the international university rankings. It's just as obvious that as a rich country, we have the prerequisites to be in line with our Nordic neighbouring countries. The Norwegian contribution to the global research effort is less than one percent, and a population of five million people has their limitations. But does that mean that we should leave resource-intensive and ground-breaking research to major players? I mean no. First, we're already among the very best in some areas. For example, we're one of the world's leading seamates. Here's what we've done for a large part of the breakthroughs. Because we are a country with limited resources, we need to sharpen our efforts and concentrate on asserting ourselves internationally in areas where we have extra good assumptions. Getting really good in some areas requires stable frameworks and good coherence between investment in construction, students and researchers. Therefore, we are starting with a long-term plan for research and higher education, which has a 10-year perspective, and which is an important instrument for prioritisation in order to achieve a clearer level of ambition in different areas. It is a very important step in the research report that has been presented. The major universities express ambitions to strengthen themselves internationally. The question is whether you're hungry enough in our oil-smelted society to achieve those ambitions. In many other countries, there are major changes in research systems. In Europe, research resources are concentrated. New and emerging research nations in Asia and Latin America are playing a heavy role and will certainly have an increasing impact on the global knowledge pool. In particular, Norway must pursue policies that support the international ambitions of the major universities. It's not enough to run faster yourself, if all of the others also run faster. It will benefit Norway as a society to have one or more universities that succeed better internationally. The Ministry of Knowledge will therefore focus on the professional development of some of our most research-intensive universities and compare them with the best universities in the Nordic region. The aim is to develop knowledge that can support the institutions, the Research Council and the Ministry in their efforts to improve the quality of research and education. Universities and higher education policies are in continuous development. From a longer time perspective, major changes have taken place in the sector, including a stronger focus on quality. I believe that even more profiling and spicing of the institutions is needed to achieve a quality level in the sector. Increased profiling involves choosing the professional centre of gravity and level of ambition adapted to each institution. Warloe is asking for measures to lift our best universities. We should have such measures, but I am also committed to measures that can lift all the institutions, each based on their assumptions. In particular, I would like to highlight three measures: the first is the new target structure from 2012 for universities and higher education. There is one important measure to stimulate increased profiling and differentiation. The target structure provides for less detailed management by the Ministry and gives institutions room for action and responsibility for setting objectives that are in line with their specificity and ambitions. It shall provide greater scope for specialisation in fields where institutions have specific prerequisites for success. Institutions have great freedom within the framework of collapse and government, and must define their strengths and objectives. In the international context, Norwegian universities and higher education institutions also have a high level of government funding. Given the freedom of the institutions, it follows a similar great responsibility to set its own priorities in order to achieve the overall objectives. Another important measure to improve the quality of the UH sector is the performance-based census of indicators in research and education. In general terms, the funding mechanism for universities and colleges has remained unchanged for almost ten years. The system is the same for all institutions and has produced good results in one way, but may also have contributed to making Norwegian universities and colleges more similar. In the research report, we have therefore announced a comprehensive review of the funding of universities and higher education institutions. In this we need, among other things: assess the relationship between direct appropriations and external research funding. There is also a need to assess results-based funding towards funding that is based more on policy objectives and institutions’ strategies. The question here is, among other things, whether the systems are now good enough to reach longer in terms of quality. The Norwegian Research Council is a key instrument for improving the quality of Norwegian research. The evaluation of the Research Council gives a positive main impression of the organisation, but points to challenges in supporting the truly bold and marginal research. The Research Council will therefore focus on innovation and the potential for cross-border research more strongly when designing its instruments and assessment systems. At the same time, we must continue the quality instruments in the Research Council which have been shown to produce results. One such instrument is the centres for excellent research. Before Christmas last year, 13 new research environments were designated SFF after hard competition. These centres illustrate that the focus of Norwegian initiatives in research is not the institutions but the professional communities. At our universities and colleges there will be some excellent environments, while others are more average, and the excellents don't necessarily have to be just at the largest institutions. Nevertheless, the majority of the new centres are located at our major universities, the ones that initially have the greatest opportunity to take off internationally. Concentration of such environments on some institutions will contribute to making these institutions more visible internationally. At the same time, the Ministry has wanted to highlight good education. That is why we are now financing four centres of excellence. It will be very exciting to see how these centres gradually divide between institutions. As mentioned at the outset, we must not forget that universities and colleges have other purposes in addition to creating internationally visible research institutions. Universities and higher education institutions deliver candidates in all areas of education both nationally and to their regions. Without good professionals, such as teachers, nurses and engineers, public services and Norwegian business will suffer. Without capable economists and social suites, municipalities and public Norway will stop. We need to maintain knowledge environments throughout the country. A well-developed provision of higher education is important in meeting regional policy objectives. We have to be able to balance these considerations at the same time.

We believe that we need to look at the practical share of the new teacher training and see if you can increase that proportion somewhat. It is also important that careers and career paths allow teachers to develop in school. Continuing education is then important. It will be important to have a mandatory mentoring system and it will also be important to have more groups of professionals in school so that teachers can actually pursue the profession in which they are trained and set up. Today, I think a lot of teachers are experiencing that they get tasks that they didn't intend to be part of in the teaching profession, in meeting with the school. ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-21.ana.ud1877e3407	At the request of the Church, Education and Research Committee, the President will propose that speaking time be limited to 40 minutes and allocated by up to 5 minutes to each party and up to 5 minutes to a member of the Government. ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-21.ana.ud1877e3416	It is important that all parents and children have the opportunity to be as together as possible in the first year. It's true for those who are at work, and it's true for those who are students. With good organisation, it is possible to adapt life as a student also to having responsibility for children. Compared to other countries, Norway has good arrangements for students who have children. There are several special schemes for students with children in the current education support scheme. Students who have children under education are given parental leave, who have many similarities with the parental pension scheme in their working life. This is a scheme in which all educational support is provided as a grant up to 44 weeks after birth. Student parents have the freedom to distribute this period between themselves. Students with children also have the right to a dependant grant in addition to normal education support. In 2008, the first paragraph of Section 4-5 of the Higher Education Act was amended to ensure equal rights for all students who have children during their studies. This provision entitles all students to leave from the studies during pregnancy and to care for children. Furthermore, it is clarified in the law that the student still has the status of student at the institution while he is at home with leave, and he also has the right to resume his studies at the same level as before leave. This has been important for how institutions behave towards students who have children. This provision also allows the Ministry to provide a regulation on the rules for the postpartum examination, and some institutions already have local rules to that effect. By law, universities and colleges are obliged to facilitate the resumption of postgraduate studies as soon as possible after birth. In 2008, changes were also made to student relations regulations, so that students on maternity leave can choose to pay holiday fees, giving access to the many welfare services offered by the student community even during the leave period. Education support must also be seen in the context of other welfare benefits for students with children, such as cheaper dorms, student housing and student rebates, one-off maternity benefits, child allowances, child allowances and transitional benefits for lone dependants. We have noticed that parents are given parental leave during the summer months to those who have children during the summer after completing education. A large proportion of students who have worked alongside the studies will be entitled to parental leave equivalent to the income they have had, and there is reason to believe that some students who have children at the end of the studies will have had income to receive parental leave from Nav. Nevertheless, there is a group, despite all these schemes, which are at the intersection of two welfare schemes and which could be at risk of falling between two chairs. It is important to improve the situation for those who have children between studies and work. Therefore, this issue in the border area between different welfare systems should be addressed.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-21.ana.ud1877e3433	We in the Progress Party are committed to ensuring that the country's students have good arrangements that contribute to the proper functioning of the study situation. We need to ensure that we have good framework conditions, well-functioning financial arrangements and flexible solutions, which allow students to focus their everyday lives on acquiring knowledge in the study rather than other things. But at the same time, we also believe that equal rights to higher education are important. That means that we have to make sure that anyone who wants and is able to do so is given the opportunity to take higher education. This requires, for example: good financial assumptions for students today. Here, of course, student loans and scholarships are important in order to achieve the goal we have in the Progress Party of reintroducing the full-time student and ensuring that everyone, regardless of the economic background, actually gets the opportunity to study full-time. But apart from the desire of the Progress Party and the struggle for higher student funding, there is also another important topic that we are dealing with here, namely the economic situation of students who either have children before, have children during the study period or become pregnant during the study period and are born immediately afterwards. As the representative Yrvin had in mind, we have a lot of good arrangements today for students who have children or have children during the study period – everything from good leave arrangements, extra financial support, the organisation of examination discharges, student children's gardens, etc. – which help to enable women to take higher education regardless of their situation in life and family. It is incredibly important to ensure that everyone has equal rights and access to education. But one thing that is far from good enough is the arrangements for those students who get pregnant at the end of the course and give birth immediately afterwards. The representative Yrvin was concerned that this is a challenge we have to look at further, but that's not good enough. Here we have a group that is not large – there are a few that apply – but just as fully it is incredibly important to make sure that this small group actually gets good financial arrangements that enable them to survive financially. The situation today is such that those who are at work, full-time workers, etc., have their rights through the fear of the people and receive financial support at birth through it. Furthermore, there are those in the middle of the course who have access to financial support schemes through the Loan Fund and who receive their support. Unfortunately, those who give birth shortly after the end of the course, before six months, do not have access to the financial arrangements either through popular interest or through the Loan Fund. Then we see that there are a few students falling between two chairs, and they end up in a situation where they don't get any financial support. Then it's not good enough to look further at this. In fact, you have to look at the first proposal that the left comes with, that is to say, the need to secure financial support for the students concerned.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-21.ana.ud1877e3519	The representative, Dagrun Eriksen, then addressed the proposals she referred to.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-21.ana.ud1877e3522	This theme is not an unknown theme. The Committee has discussed this in many rounds in the past. There is a problem that the student organisations have probably raised with everyone here in the hall and with me. It is important to provide facilities for students who have children during their studies, and to strengthen education support for students with children. It is mentioned as an objective in Soria Moria II, which is therefore the declaration of this government. Luckily, we already have good loan arrangements in the Loan Fund regulations for students who have children: Student parents can receive full training support as a grant for 44 weeks after birth, they can receive extra grants for support, and they have extended entitlement to sick leave in sick children, in support of delay in education and to an extended ceiling on how long the aid can be granted. All these issues have been issues in the Stortinget in the past. The government has also made some improvements, and until now we have been busy making changes for those who have special needs among student parents. Among other things, we have increased the period of entitlement to parental leave and increased the age limit of entitlement to sick leave when the children get sick. In the current budget, we also received a few minor measures that are important for a few who previously risked standing without rights. This applies to those who are born before term and to students who are born late in the year of education and who have not applied for parental leave in the ordinary application for training. I believe it is necessary to continue working to improve education support for students with children. At the same time, I want to stress that students with children are a large group of different economies and different needs for adaptations. The new measures should be targeted in order to meet those students with the greatest need. The aim is for students to have a financial means to have children while they are studying and to be able at the same time to complete the education without undue delay. Students who have children either just before or shortly after the training period may not be at risk of receiving parental leave from the Loan Fund or parental leave from Nav. This situation is due to the fact that only work is considered as a basis for parental leave, and only training giving entitlement to support is considered as a basis for parental leave. This implies, as the Committee points out, that some persons receive only one-off benefits at birth and thus fall between two chairs. In this context, the Committee calls for this issue to be addressed in the border area between the two welfare systems. The parental allowance shall provide for students who have children while studying, so that they do not lose out financially and are therefore left with more educational loans than their fellow students. Therefore, extending the parental leave scheme to a period that continues even after the study period itself would violate the basic principle that the applicant must be a student in order to be entitled to training aid. On the other hand, the inclusion of parental leave as a basis for entitlement to parental leave by reason of ethnic origin will also be a violation of the principle that the right to parental leave has been acquired through work. If persons who have been trained are to be exempted from this principle, others may claim that there is discrimination between groups which have a relatively equal relationship with the profession. These are the dilemmas we have when we're going to evaluate solutions for those that fall between these chairs. I am absolutely in favour of proposals that can improve the situation for those who have children between studies and work and who have not developed the right to parental leave through Nav. That is why I am looking at these issues in the border area between the different welfare systems. But we have to think a little bit more about how we find solutions here, because other groups will be able to feel very quickly that they are in similar situations to students who have children, either just before or just after they finish the studies.
